    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially in the lesson videos. I’m a secondary school teacher, so there are recordings of me teaching, and when I watch them back I can’t stop seeing this one side of my face as crooked and kind of swollen-looking. And once I see it, that’s all I can see.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I don’t like how I look.” It’s more like… I become convinced that everyone else is noticing it too. The students, parents if they see the videos, other staff — I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m actually saying. It’s got to the point where it feels like it’s taking over my head, and I don’t really know how to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face been affecting your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. I wish I could say it’s just me feeling uncomfortable on camera and then I get on with the job, but it’s started spilling into almost everything I’m meant to be doing.\n\nI’m falling behind on lesson planning because I’ll sit down to prepare and then end up going back over recordings or thinking about how I came across, and suddenly the planning doesn’t get finished properly. The same thing’s happening with my mentoring responsibilities — I’m meant to be supporting other staff and students, and I keep missing things or not preparing properly because I’ve lost so much time. Even basketball coaching after school, which used to be something I was really reliable with, has become harder to keep up with.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head anymore. Administrators have started documenting missed deadlines and problems with coverage, like when I haven’t got things sorted in time or someone has to step in. That’s been humiliating, honestly, because I’ve always tried to be dependable. Now I’m worried I’m getting close to losing my position, or at least that people are starting to see me as someone who can’t manage the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts in response to your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s almost like there’s a whole routine I get trapped in, especially before anything where my face is going to be on screen.\n\nIf I’m about to post a recorded lesson, I’ll start by checking the camera angle, and then I’ll change it slightly because I think one side of my face looks worse from that direction. Then I’ll replay a short clip, and I won’t even be listening to what I’m teaching anymore — I’m just watching my face. I’ll pause it, rewind it, watch the same few seconds again and again, sometimes with my hand partly covering the side I’m worried about, just to see if it looks less obvious. And if it still looks wrong, I’ll re-record the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I can lose so much time doing that. The same thing happens before parent conferences online. I’ll adjust the laptop, move closer, move back, tilt the screen, check how my face looks, then check again. I know I should be preparing what I’m actually going to say, but I get caught up trying to make it look less noticeable.\n\nThere have been times I’ve re-recorded a lesson so many times that I end up missing the prep I was supposed to do for the next class, or I’m late getting things ready. It’s not just one quick check. It can take over the whole block of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and re-recording helps reduce your worry about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Only very briefly. That’s the problem, I think. In the moment it feels like if I just check it one more time, or shift the camera slightly, then I’ll feel settled enough to post the lesson or join the meeting. And sometimes there’s a tiny bit of relief, like, “Okay, maybe that angle hides it a bit better.”\n\nBut it never really lasts. I end up replaying the same clip again, covering part of my face with my hand, changing the angle, watching it back, and then finding something else wrong. Before a parent conference I’ll do the same thing with the laptop — move it up, tilt it down, check again — and I can waste so much time that I’m not actually preparing what I need to say.\n\nWith recorded lessons, it’s even worse because I can re-record the whole thing over and over. I tell myself the next version will be the one I can tolerate, but then I watch it and get stuck again. So it’s not really helping. It just makes me late with prep and sometimes late with classes, and then I feel worse because I’ve lost control of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specifically 'not right' or uneven about your face, beyond just it looking crooked or swollen?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “crooked” and “swollen” are the words I keep using because they’re the closest way to explain it, but it feels more specific than just a vague bad angle. It’s like one side of my face looks wrong to me — heavier, puffier, sort of pulled out of balance compared with the other side. Once I see it on a lesson video, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd then I’m not thinking, “Oh, that was a decent explanation,” or “The students will understand the material.” I’m thinking, “That side of my face looks distorted,” and I feel absolutely certain that the students are noticing it too. Or parents, if it’s a conference. Or staff, if they see a recording or I’m in a meeting. I imagine them staring at that part of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nIt’s not just that I dislike how I look in general. It’s this particular uneven, swollen-looking side that catches my attention every time, and then everything else about the lesson disappears for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you excessively concerned about any parts of your body other than your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really… at least not in the same way. It’s my face. That’s what I’m here about. I can’t stop seeing this one side as crooked or swollen-looking, especially when I’m watching myself back on lesson videos.\n\nOnce I notice it, it’s like everything else disappears. I’m not thinking about the teaching or whether the explanation was clear — I’m just stuck on that side of my face and how obvious it must look. And then I start imagining the students seeing it too, or parents during conferences, or staff if they watch something I’ve recorded. I feel like they must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s specifically my face, and it feels impossible to move past once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your face, or do you feel that your view of it is absolute?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I could say yes, because part of me knows you’re probably asking whether I can see that it might be my perception or that maybe I’m being too fixed on it. And I do try to tell myself that. I’ll think, “Maybe it’s just the lighting,” or “Maybe cameras distort things,” or “Maybe nobody else sees it the way I do.”\n\nBut when I actually see my face — especially on screen — it doesn’t feel like an opinion. It feels like I’m just looking at something obvious. Like, I can argue with myself in words, but visually it still looks wrong to me. So it’s hard to believe I could just change my mind about it, because in the moment it feels less like a thought and more like evidence sitting right in front of me.\n\nAt the same time… I suppose I wouldn’t be here if I thought there was absolutely no possibility that my view could be off in some way. I don’t know. That’s the confusing part. I can admit, sitting here, that maybe my reaction is too strong or maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people do. But when I’m actually confronted with my face, it feels very fixed. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because they notice something wrong with it, or because they are judging you as a person?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that they’re staring because they notice something wrong with my face. That’s what it feels like, anyway. Like they’ve seen that one side looks crooked or swollen or just… off, and then they can’t stop looking at it.\n\nI don’t usually think, “They’re judging me as a whole person,” exactly. It’s not like I’m imagining them thinking I’m a bad teacher or a bad person because of it. It’s more immediate than that. I’m standing there teaching, or watching myself on a lesson video afterward, and all I can focus on is that side of my face. Then I start feeling certain that the students must have been seeing the same thing instead of listening to the lesson.\n\nAnd with parents or staff, it’s the same kind of thing. If I’m in a conference or someone sees a recording, I picture them noticing that uneven, swollen-looking side and being distracted by it. Like my face becomes the whole thing they’re paying attention to, not what I’m saying. So… yes, I’d say it’s about them noticing something wrong with my appearance more than judging my character.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations where you avoid interacting with others because of concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, it’s mostly situations where I feel like my face is going to be looked at closely, or where I can’t control what angle people are seeing me from.\n\nAt school, it can be really hard. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it, it feels like that’s all anyone else could see too. So if I know there’s going to be a parent meeting, or staff are going to be watching something I’ve recorded, I start wanting to avoid it or keep it as brief as possible. I’ll think, “They’re not listening to what I’m saying, they’re just staring at that side of my face.”\n\nWith students it’s complicated, because obviously I can’t just avoid teaching. That’s my job. But I do find myself dreading certain interactions more, especially if I feel exposed or if I’ve recently watched myself on video and seen it again. Then I’m standing there in front of the class, but part of me is not really present — I’m thinking about whether they can see it, whether they’re looking at it, whether it’s distracting them.\n\nSo yes, I guess I avoid some interactions when I can, especially with parents or staff, or anything recorded. And when I can’t avoid them, I’m still kind of trapped in the thought that they must be staring at my face instead of paying attention to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides teaching and interactions with students, parents, and staff, where you feel your concerns about your face are causing problems or difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s not just the actual teaching moments anymore. It’s started affecting the parts of my job that happen outside the classroom, and honestly that might be where it’s becoming most obvious to other people.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on lesson planning because I’ll sit down to work and then somehow I get pulled back into thinking about the videos or how I looked that day, and I lose so much time. Or I’ll avoid dealing with anything connected to a recorded lesson because I don’t want to see myself again. Then the planning doesn’t get finished when it should. It’s not that I don’t care — I do care, a lot — but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nMentoring has been affected too. I’m supposed to be supporting other staff and following through on things, but I’ve missed things or delayed them because I’m distracted or trying to avoid situations where I feel like I’m going to be seen too closely. And basketball coaching… that’s been slipping as well. I used to be much more reliable with practices and coverage and organizing things, but now even that feels harder to stay on top of.\n\nThe worst part is that administrators have noticed. It’s not just in my head that my work is slipping. They’ve started documenting missed deadlines and coverage problems, and that’s terrifying, because I feel like I’m getting close to losing my position. I keep thinking, “How did it get to this point?” I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I know it sounds like I should just finish the work, but so much of my time and energy gets eaten up by this that things just don’t get done on time anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would lessen if you were completely alone and had no interactions with others?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would lessen some if I were completely alone, because there wouldn’t be that immediate fear of someone else seeing it. Like if there were no students in front of me, no parents, no staff, no lesson videos that other people might watch, then I wouldn’t be thinking quite as much, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think it would just disappear. Because the problem starts when I see it myself too. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it sort of locks in. Then even when I’m not actually with people, I’m replaying how I must have looked and imagining what everyone else noticed.\n\nSo if I were alone, maybe the panic about being watched would be lower in that moment. But the concern is still there because I’d still know it looks wrong to me, and I’d still be thinking about what people would see the next time I had to be around them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your face if you were certain that no one else noticed or cared about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes. Maybe it would take away part of it, the part where I’m imagining students or parents or staff staring at me instead of listening. If I could somehow know, like really know, that nobody noticed or cared, maybe I wouldn’t feel quite as exposed in front of people.\n\nBut the problem is that I notice it. That’s the part I can’t get around. I’m here because I keep seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it’s like I can’t unsee it. Even if someone told me, “No one else sees that,” I don’t know that I’d believe it fully, because to me it looks so obvious. It feels like it’s right there on the screen, and then my mind immediately goes to, “How could they not be looking at that?”\n\nSo… yes, I think I would still be concerned. Maybe I’d be less panicked about what other people think, but I’d still be stuck on how it looks to me. And then I’d probably still end up thinking about students, parents, and staff anyway, because if I can see it that clearly, it feels impossible that they wouldn’t be staring at it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the perceived flaw in your face isn't actually there, or that it's not as noticeable to others as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know I’m supposed to say there’s a possibility, because logically I understand people might not be seeing it the way I am. I’m not completely unaware of that idea. But emotionally, when I’m looking at the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel questionable. It feels obvious.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. I can’t stop seeing this one side of my face as crooked or swollen-looking, and once I notice it, it’s like everything else in the video disappears. I’m supposed to be looking at whether the lesson went well, or whether I explained something clearly, but instead I’m staring at my face and thinking, “That looks wrong. That’s what everyone must be seeing.”\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole loop where I imagine the students noticing it while I’m teaching, or parents noticing it if they watch something, or staff seeing it and focusing on that instead of anything I’m saying. So… could it be not as noticeable to others? Maybe. I can say maybe. But I don’t really feel convinced of that, because to me it looks so clear once I see it. That’s the problem — I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face is more about how you feel about your own appearance or about how others might evaluate you as a person because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with how I see myself. Like, I’m not first thinking, “They’ll think I’m a bad person,” exactly. It’s more that I see that one side of my face on the lesson videos and it looks crooked, or swollen, or just… wrong, and then I can’t move past it.\n\nBut then almost immediately it becomes about other people too. Because I’m a teacher, I’m in front of students all day, and there are parents and staff who might see me or see videos, and once I’ve noticed it, I feel like they must be noticing it too. I’ll think, “How could they be listening to what I’m saying if that’s what my face looks like?” It’s like I imagine them staring at that instead of focusing on the lesson.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s exactly that I think they’re judging my whole character because of it. It’s more like I feel exposed and embarrassed, like this thing on my face is so obvious that it takes over how people see me. Even if they’re not saying anything, I picture them noticing it, maybe being distracted by it, and then I can’t concentrate on anything else either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and re-record your lessons if you were the only person who would ever see them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably would, which is embarrassing to admit because if I’m the only one seeing it, then it should matter less, right? But I don’t think it would completely stop.\n\nWhen I’m recording lessons, before I post anything or even before something like a parent conference, I get stuck on how my face looks on the camera. I’ll adjust the angle over and over, like moving the laptop a little higher, then lower, then turning slightly to one side, trying to find the version where that side of my face doesn’t look so noticeable. And then I’ll replay little clips again and again, not really listening to what I’m teaching, but watching my face. Sometimes I’ll literally cover part of my face with my hand while I’m watching, just to see if it looks better or to compare what I think is wrong.\n\nSo if I were the only person who would ever see the video… I think I’d still check it, because I would still see it. Maybe I wouldn’t be as frantic about posting it, but I’d still feel this pull to fix it somehow or re-record it so it looks less awful to me. There have been times I’ve re-recorded the whole thing again and again because I can’t stand how it looks, and then I lose so much time that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to my next class. It becomes this whole thing where I tell myself, “Just one more take,” but it’s never just one more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you received reassurance from others that you look fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but I don’t think it would really fix it. Like, if someone said, “You look fine,” I might nod and try to believe them, and part of me would want that to be enough. But then the next time I see myself on a lesson video, I’m right back to staring at that side of my face and thinking it looks crooked or swollen-looking.\n\nIt’s not just that I need someone to tell me I’m okay. It’s that once I notice it, it feels so obvious to me that reassurance almost doesn’t land. I start thinking, “They’re just being kind,” or “Maybe they didn’t look closely,” or “They wouldn’t tell me if it really looked bad.” And then I imagine the students seeing it while I’m teaching, or parents or staff watching and focusing on that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… I think reassurance might calm me down briefly, but it doesn’t stop me from seeing it. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t seem to get past that image of my face once it’s in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Sometimes I’ll literally cover part of my face with my hand while I’m watching, just to see if it looks better or to compare what I think is wrong.\n\nSo if I were the only person who would ever see the video… I think I’d still check it, because I would still see it. Maybe I wouldn’t be as frantic about posting it, but I’d still feel this pull to fix it somehow or re-record it so it looks less awful to me. There have been times I’ve re-recorded the whole thing again and again because I can’t stand how it looks, and then I lose so much time that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to my next class. It becomes this whole thing where I tell myself, “Just one more take,” but it’s never just one more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you received reassurance from others that you look fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but I don’t think it would really fix it. Like, if someone said, “You look fine,” I might nod and try to believe them, and part of me would want that to be enough. But then the next time I see myself on a lesson video, I’m right back to staring at that side of my face and thinking it looks crooked or swollen-looking.\n\nIt’s not just that I need someone to tell me I’m okay. It’s that once I notice it, it feels so obvious to me that reassurance almost doesn’t land. I start thinking, “They’re just being kind,” or “Maybe they didn’t look closely,” or “They wouldn’t tell me if it really looked bad.” And then I imagine the students seeing it while I’m teaching, or parents or staff watching and focusing on that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… I think reassurance might calm me down briefly, but it doesn’t stop me from seeing it. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t seem to get past that image of my face once it’s in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your concerns about your face would still exist if you lived in complete isolation with no possibility of social interaction?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… that’s hard to imagine, honestly. If I were completely isolated, with no students, no parents, no staff, nobody watching a lesson video or looking at me in a hallway, maybe part of the panic about other people noticing it would be less. I wouldn’t have that immediate thought of, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”

But I don’t think the concern would disappear, because the first part of it is still me seeing it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and once I’ve seen it, it sort of becomes the only thing I can focus on. Even if no one else were around, I think I’d still look at my face and feel like something was wrong with it.

The social part makes it worse, definitely. Being a teacher makes it feel so exposed, like my face is right there in front of everyone and they must be noticing it. But even without that, I don’t think I’d suddenly feel normal about it. I think I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially in the lesson videos. I’m a secondary school teacher, so there are recordings of me teaching, and when I watch them back I can’t stop seeing this one side of my face as crooked and kind of swollen-looking. And once I see it, that’s all I can see.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I don’t like how I look.” It’s more like… I become convinced that everyone else is noticing it too. The students, parents if they see the videos, other staff — I feel like they must be staring at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m actually saying. It’s got to the point where it feels like it’s taking over my head, and I don’t really know how to get past it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face been affecting your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. I wish I could say it’s just me feeling uncomfortable on camera and then I get on with the job, but it’s started spilling into almost everything I’m meant to be doing.\n\nI’m falling behind on lesson planning because I’ll sit down to prepare and then end up going back over recordings or thinking about how I came across, and suddenly the planning doesn’t get finished properly. The same thing’s happening with my mentoring responsibilities — I’m meant to be supporting other staff and students, and I keep missing things or not preparing properly because I’ve lost so much time. Even basketball coaching after school, which used to be something I was really reliable with, has become harder to keep up with.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head anymore. It’s almost like there’s a whole routine I get trapped in, especially before anything where my face is going to be on screen.\n\nIf I’m about to post a recorded lesson, I’ll start by checking the camera angle, and then I’ll change it slightly because I think one side of my face looks worse from that direction. Then I’ll replay a short clip, and I won’t even be listening to what I’m teaching anymore — I’m just watching my face. I’ll pause it, rewind it, watch the same few seconds again and again, sometimes with my hand partly covering the side I’m worried about, just to see if it looks less obvious. And if it still looks wrong, I’ll re-record the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I can lose so much time doing that. The same thing happens before parent conferences online. I’ll adjust the laptop, move closer, move back, tilt the screen, check how my face looks, then check again. I know I should be preparing what I’m actually going to say, but I get caught up trying to make it look less noticeable.\n\nThere have been times I’ve re-recorded a lesson so many times that I end up missing the prep I was supposed to do for the next class, or I’m late getting things ready. It’s not just one quick check. It can take over the whole block of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and re-recording helps reduce your worry about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Only very briefly. That’s the problem, I think. In the moment it feels like if I just check it one more time, or shift the camera slightly, then I’ll feel settled enough to post the lesson or join the meeting. And sometimes there’s a tiny bit of relief, like, “Okay, maybe that angle hides it a bit better.”\n\nBut it never really lasts. I end up replaying the same clip again, covering part of my face with my hand, changing the angle, watching it back, and then finding something else wrong. Before a parent conference I’ll do the same thing with the laptop — move it up, tilt it down, check again — and I can waste so much time that I’m not actually preparing what I need to say.\n\nWith recorded lessons, it’s even worse because I can re-record the whole thing over and over. I tell myself the next version will be the one I can tolerate, but then I watch it and get stuck again. So it’s not really helping. It just makes me late with prep and sometimes late with classes, and then I feel worse because I’ve lost control of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specifically 'not right' or uneven about your face, beyond just it looking crooked or swollen?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “crooked” and “swollen” are the words I keep using because they’re the closest way to explain it, but it feels more specific than just a vague bad angle. It’s like one side of my face looks wrong to me — heavier, puffier, sort of pulled out of balance compared with the other side. Once I see it on a lesson video, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd then I’m not thinking, “Oh, that was a decent explanation,” or “The students will understand the material.” I’m thinking, “That side of my face looks distorted,” and I feel absolutely certain that the students are noticing it too. Or parents, if it’s a conference. Or staff, if they see a recording or I’m in a meeting. I imagine them staring at that part of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nIt’s not just that I dislike how I look in general. It’s this particular uneven, swollen-looking side that catches my attention every time, and then everything else about the lesson disappears for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you excessively concerned about any parts of your body other than your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really… at least not in the same way. It’s my face. That’s what I’m here about. I can’t stop seeing this one side as crooked or swollen-looking, especially when I’m watching myself back on lesson videos.\n\nOnce I notice it, it’s like everything else disappears. I’m not thinking about the teaching or whether the explanation was clear — I’m just stuck on that side of my face and how obvious it must look. And then I start imagining the students seeing it too, or parents during conferences, or staff if they watch something I’ve recorded. I feel like they must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s specifically my face, and it feels impossible to move past once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your face, or do you feel that your view of it is absolute?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I could say yes, because part of me knows you’re probably asking whether I can see that it might be my perception or that maybe I’m being too fixed on it. And I do try to tell myself that. I’ll think, “Maybe it’s just the lighting,” or “Maybe cameras distort things,” or “Maybe nobody else sees it the way I do.”\n\nBut when I actually see my face — especially on screen — it doesn’t feel like an opinion. It feels like I’m just looking at something obvious. Like, I can argue with myself in words, but visually it still looks wrong to me. So it’s hard to believe I could just change my mind about it, because in the moment it feels less like a thought and more like evidence sitting right in front of me.\n\nAt the same time… I suppose I wouldn’t be here if I thought there was absolutely no possibility that my view could be off in some way. I don’t know. That’s the confusing part. I can admit, sitting here, that maybe my reaction is too strong or maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people do. But when I’m actually confronted with my face, it feels very fixed. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because they notice something wrong with it, or because they are judging you as a person?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that they’re staring because they notice something wrong with my face. That’s what it feels like, anyway. Like they’ve seen that one side looks crooked or swollen or just… off, and then they can’t stop looking at it.\n\nI don’t usually think, “They’re judging me as a whole person,” exactly. It’s not like I’m imagining them thinking I’m a bad teacher or a bad person because of it. It’s more immediate than that. I’m standing there teaching, or watching myself on a lesson video afterward, and all I can focus on is that side of my face. Then I start feeling certain that the students must have been seeing the same thing instead of listening to the lesson.\n\nAnd with parents or staff, it’s the same kind of thing. If I’m in a conference or someone sees a recording, I picture them noticing that uneven, swollen-looking side and being distracted by it. Like my face becomes the whole thing they’re paying attention to, not what I’m saying. So… yes, I’d say it’s about them noticing something wrong with my appearance more than judging my character.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations where you avoid interacting with others because of concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, it’s mostly situations where I feel like my face is going to be looked at closely, or where I can’t control what angle people are seeing me from.\n\nAt school, it can be really hard. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it, it feels like that’s all anyone else could see too. So if I know there’s going to be a parent meeting, or staff are going to be watching something I’ve recorded, I start wanting to avoid it or keep it as brief as possible. I’ll think, “They’re not listening to what I’m saying, they’re just staring at that side of my face.”\n\nWith students it’s complicated, because obviously I can’t just avoid teaching. That’s my job. Then I’m standing there in front of the class, but part of me is not really present — I’m thinking about whether they can see it, whether they’re looking at it, whether it’s distracting them.\n\nSo yes, I guess I avoid some interactions when I can, especially with parents or staff, or anything recorded. And when I can’t avoid them, I’m still kind of trapped in the thought that they must be staring at my face instead of paying attention to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides teaching and interactions with students, parents, and staff, where you feel your concerns about your face are causing problems or difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s not just the actual teaching moments anymore. It’s started affecting the parts of my job that happen outside the classroom, and honestly that might be where it’s becoming most obvious to other people.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on lesson planning because I’ll sit down to work and then somehow I get pulled back into thinking about the videos or how I looked that day, and I lose so much time. Or I’ll avoid dealing with anything connected to a recorded lesson because I don’t want to see myself again. Then the planning doesn’t get finished when it should. It’s not that I don’t care — I do care, a lot — but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nMentoring has been affected too. I keep thinking, “How did it get to this point?” I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I know it sounds like I should just finish the work, but so much of my time and energy gets eaten up by this that things just don’t get done on time anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would lessen if you were completely alone and had no interactions with others?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would lessen some if I were completely alone, because there wouldn’t be that immediate fear of someone else seeing it. Like if there were no students in front of me, no parents, no staff, no lesson videos that other people might watch, then I wouldn’t be thinking quite as much, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think it would just disappear. Because the problem starts when I see it myself too. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it sort of locks in. Then even when I’m not actually with people, I’m replaying how I must have looked and imagining what everyone else noticed.\n\nSo if I were alone, maybe the panic about being watched would be lower in that moment. But the concern is still there because I’d still know it looks wrong to me, and I’d still be thinking about what people would see the next time I had to be around them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your face if you were certain that no one else noticed or cared about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes. Maybe it would take away part of it, the part where I’m imagining students or parents or staff staring at me instead of listening. If I could somehow know, like really know, that nobody noticed or cared, maybe I wouldn’t feel quite as exposed in front of people.\n\nBut the problem is that I notice it. That’s the part I can’t get around. I’m here because I keep seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it’s like I can’t unsee it. Even if someone told me, “No one else sees that,” I don’t know that I’d believe it fully, because to me it looks so obvious. It feels like it’s right there on the screen, and then my mind immediately goes to, “How could they not be looking at that?”\n\nSo… yes, I think I would still be concerned. Maybe I’d be less panicked about what other people think, but I’d still be stuck on how it looks to me. And then I’d probably still end up thinking about students, parents, and staff anyway, because if I can see it that clearly, it feels impossible that they wouldn’t be staring at it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the perceived flaw in your face isn't actually there, or that it's not as noticeable to others as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know I’m supposed to say there’s a possibility, because logically I understand people might not be seeing it the way I am. I’m not completely unaware of that idea. But emotionally, when I’m looking at the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel questionable. It feels obvious.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. I can’t stop seeing this one side of my face as crooked or swollen-looking, and once I notice it, it’s like everything else in the video disappears. I’m supposed to be looking at whether the lesson went well, or whether I explained something clearly, but instead I’m staring at my face and thinking, “That looks wrong. That’s what everyone must be seeing.”\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole loop where I imagine the students noticing it while I’m teaching, or parents noticing it if they watch something, or staff seeing it and focusing on that instead of anything I’m saying. So… could it be not as noticeable to others? Maybe. I can say maybe. But I don’t really feel convinced of that, because to me it looks so clear once I see it. That’s the problem — I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face is more about how you feel about your own appearance or about how others might evaluate you as a person because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with how I see myself. Like, I’m not first thinking, “They’ll think I’m a bad person,” exactly. It’s more that I see that one side of my face on the lesson videos and it looks crooked, or swollen, or just… wrong, and then I can’t move past it.\n\nBut then almost immediately it becomes about other people too. Because I’m a teacher, I’m in front of students all day, and there are parents and staff who might see me or see videos, and once I’ve noticed it, I feel like they must be noticing it too. I’ll think, “How could they be listening to what I’m saying if that’s what my face looks like?” It’s like I imagine them staring at that instead of focusing on the lesson.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s exactly that I think they’re judging my whole character because of it. It’s more like I feel exposed and embarrassed, like this thing on my face is so obvious that it takes over how people see me. Even if they’re not saying anything, I picture them noticing it, maybe being distracted by it, and then I can’t concentrate on anything else either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and re-record your lessons if you were the only person who would ever see them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably would, which is embarrassing to admit because if I’m the only one seeing it, then it should matter less, right? But I don’t think it would completely stop.\n\nWhen I’m recording lessons, before I post anything or even before something like a parent conference, I get stuck on how my face looks on the camera. I’ll adjust the angle over and over, like moving the laptop a little higher, then lower, then turning slightly to one side, trying to find the version where that side of my face doesn’t look so noticeable. And then I’ll replay little clips again and again, not really listening to what I’m teaching, but watching my face. Sometimes I’ll literally cover part of my face with my hand while I’m watching, just to see if it looks better or to compare what I think is wrong.\n\nSo if I were the only person who would ever see the video… I think I’d still check it, because I would still see it. Maybe I wouldn’t be as frantic about posting it, but I’d still feel this pull to fix it somehow or re-record it so it looks less awful to me. There have been times I’ve re-recorded the whole thing again and again because I can’t stand how it looks, and then I lose so much time that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to my next class. It becomes this whole thing where I tell myself, “Just one more take,” but it’s never just one more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you received reassurance from others that you look fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but I don’t think it would really fix it. Like, if someone said, “You look fine,” I might nod and try to believe them, and part of me would want that to be enough. But then the next time I see myself on a lesson video, I’m right back to staring at that side of my face and thinking it looks crooked or swollen-looking.\n\nIt’s not just that I need someone to tell me I’m okay. It’s that once I notice it, it feels so obvious to me that reassurance almost doesn’t land. I start thinking, “They’re just being kind,” or “Maybe they didn’t look closely,” or “They wouldn’t tell me if it really looked bad.” And then I imagine the students seeing it while I’m teaching, or parents or staff watching and focusing on that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… I think reassurance might calm me down briefly, but it doesn’t stop me from seeing it. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t seem to get past that image of my face once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would still exist if you lived in complete isolation with no possibility of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I think… that’s hard to imagine, honestly. If I were completely isolated, with no students, no parents, no staff, nobody watching a lesson video or looking at me in a hallway, maybe part of the panic about other people noticing it would be less. I wouldn’t have that immediate thought of, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think the concern would disappear, because the first part of it is still me seeing it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and once I’ve seen it, it sort of becomes the only thing I can focus on. Even if no one else were around, I think I’d still look at my face and feel like something was wrong with it.\n\nThe social part makes it worse, definitely. Being a teacher makes it feel so exposed, like my face is right there in front of everyone and they must be noticing it. But even without that, I don’t think I’d suddenly feel normal about it. I think I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s almost like there’s a whole routine I get trapped in, especially before anything where my face is going to be on screen.\n\nIf I’m about to post a recorded lesson, I’ll start by checking the camera angle, and then I’ll change it slightly because I think one side of my face looks worse from that direction. Then I’ll replay a short clip, and I won’t even be listening to what I’m teaching anymore — I’m just watching my face. I’ll pause it, rewind it, watch the same few seconds again and again, sometimes with my hand partly covering the side I’m worried about, just to see if it looks less obvious. And if it still looks wrong, I’ll re-record the whole thing.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I can lose so much time doing that. The same thing happens before parent conferences online. I’ll adjust the laptop, move closer, move back, tilt the screen, check how my face looks, then check again. I know I should be preparing what I’m actually going to say, but I get caught up trying to make it look less noticeable.\n\nThere have been times I’ve re-recorded a lesson so many times that I end up missing the prep I was supposed to do for the next class, or I’m late getting things ready. It’s not just one quick check. It can take over the whole block of time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and re-recording helps reduce your worry about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Only very briefly. That’s the problem, I think. In the moment it feels like if I just check it one more time, or shift the camera slightly, then I’ll feel settled enough to post the lesson or join the meeting. And sometimes there’s a tiny bit of relief, like, “Okay, maybe that angle hides it a bit better.”\n\nBut it never really lasts. I end up replaying the same clip again, covering part of my face with my hand, changing the angle, watching it back, and then finding something else wrong. Before a parent conference I’ll do the same thing with the laptop — move it up, tilt it down, check again — and I can waste so much time that I’m not actually preparing what I need to say.\n\nWith recorded lessons, it’s even worse because I can re-record the whole thing over and over. I tell myself the next version will be the one I can tolerate, but then I watch it and get stuck again. So it’s not really helping. It just makes me late with prep and sometimes late with classes, and then I feel worse because I’ve lost control of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specifically 'not right' or uneven about your face, beyond just it looking crooked or swollen?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “crooked” and “swollen” are the words I keep using because they’re the closest way to explain it, but it feels more specific than just a vague bad angle. It’s like one side of my face looks wrong to me — heavier, puffier, sort of pulled out of balance compared with the other side. Once I see it on a lesson video, I can’t unsee it.\n\nAnd then I’m not thinking, “Oh, that was a decent explanation,” or “The students will understand the material.” I’m thinking, “That side of my face looks distorted,” and I feel absolutely certain that the students are noticing it too. Or parents, if it’s a conference. Or staff, if they see a recording or I’m in a meeting. I imagine them staring at that part of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nIt’s not just that I dislike how I look in general. It’s this particular uneven, swollen-looking side that catches my attention every time, and then everything else about the lesson disappears for me.\n\nDoctor: Are you excessively concerned about any parts of your body other than your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really… at least not in the same way. It’s my face. That’s what I’m here about. I can’t stop seeing this one side as crooked or swollen-looking, especially when I’m watching myself back on lesson videos.\n\nOnce I notice it, it’s like everything else disappears. I’m not thinking about the teaching or whether the explanation was clear — I’m just stuck on that side of my face and how obvious it must look. And then I start imagining the students seeing it too, or parents during conferences, or staff if they watch something I’ve recorded. I feel like they must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… no, it’s not really other parts of my body. It’s specifically my face, and it feels impossible to move past once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it would be possible for you to change your opinion about the appearance of your face, or do you feel that your view of it is absolute?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I could say yes, because part of me knows you’re probably asking whether I can see that it might be my perception or that maybe I’m being too fixed on it. And I do try to tell myself that. I’ll think, “Maybe it’s just the lighting,” or “Maybe cameras distort things,” or “Maybe nobody else sees it the way I do.”\n\nBut when I actually see my face — especially on screen — it doesn’t feel like an opinion. It feels like I’m just looking at something obvious. Like, I can argue with myself in words, but visually it still looks wrong to me. So it’s hard to believe I could just change my mind about it, because in the moment it feels less like a thought and more like evidence sitting right in front of me.\n\nAt the same time… I suppose I wouldn’t be here if I thought there was absolutely no possibility that my view could be off in some way. I don’t know. That’s the confusing part. I can admit, sitting here, that maybe my reaction is too strong or maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people do. But when I’m actually confronted with my face, it feels very fixed. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because they notice something wrong with it, or because they are judging you as a person?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that they’re staring because they notice something wrong with my face. That’s what it feels like, anyway. Like they’ve seen that one side looks crooked or swollen or just… off, and then they can’t stop looking at it.\n\nI don’t usually think, “They’re judging me as a whole person,” exactly. It’s not like I’m imagining them thinking I’m a bad teacher or a bad person because of it. It’s more immediate than that. I’m standing there teaching, or watching myself on a lesson video afterward, and all I can focus on is that side of my face. Then I start feeling certain that the students must have been seeing the same thing instead of listening to the lesson.\n\nAnd with parents or staff, it’s the same kind of thing. If I’m in a conference or someone sees a recording, I picture them noticing that uneven, swollen-looking side and being distracted by it. Like my face becomes the whole thing they’re paying attention to, not what I’m saying. So… yes, I’d say it’s about them noticing something wrong with my appearance more than judging my character.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations where you avoid interacting with others because of concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, it’s mostly situations where I feel like my face is going to be looked at closely, or where I can’t control what angle people are seeing me from.\n\nAt school, it can be really hard. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it, it feels like that’s all anyone else could see too. So if I know there’s going to be a parent meeting, or staff are going to be watching something I’ve recorded, I start wanting to avoid it or keep it as brief as possible. I’ll think, “They’re not listening to what I’m saying, they’re just staring at that side of my face.”\n\nWith students it’s complicated, because obviously I can’t just avoid teaching. That’s my job. But I do find myself dreading certain interactions more, especially if I feel exposed or if I’ve recently watched myself on video and seen it again. Then I’m standing there in front of the class, but part of me is not really present — I’m thinking about whether they can see it, whether they’re looking at it, whether it’s distracting them.\n\nSo yes, I guess I avoid some interactions when I can, especially with parents or staff, or anything recorded. And when I can’t avoid them, I’m still kind of trapped in the thought that they must be staring at my face instead of paying attention to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides teaching and interactions with students, parents, and staff, where you feel your concerns about your face are causing problems or difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. It’s not just the actual teaching moments anymore. It’s started affecting the parts of my job that happen outside the classroom, and honestly that might be where it’s becoming most obvious to other people.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on lesson planning because I’ll sit down to work and then somehow I get pulled back into thinking about the videos or how I looked that day, and I lose so much time. Or I’ll avoid dealing with anything connected to a recorded lesson because I don’t want to see myself again. Then the planning doesn’t get finished when it should. It’s not that I don’t care — I do care, a lot — but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nMentoring has been affected too. I’m supposed to be supporting other staff and following through on things, but I’ve missed things or delayed them because I’m distracted or trying to avoid situations where I feel like I’m going to be seen too closely. And basketball coaching… that’s been slipping as well. I used to be much more reliable with practices and coverage and organizing things, but now even that feels harder to stay on top of.\n\nThe worst part is that administrators have noticed. It’s not just in my head that my work is slipping. They’ve started documenting missed deadlines and coverage problems, and that’s terrifying, because I feel like I’m getting close to losing my position. I keep thinking, “How did it get to this point?” I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I know it sounds like I should just finish the work, but so much of my time and energy gets eaten up by this that things just don’t get done on time anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would lessen if you were completely alone and had no interactions with others?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would lessen some if I were completely alone, because there wouldn’t be that immediate fear of someone else seeing it. Like if there were no students in front of me, no parents, no staff, no lesson videos that other people might watch, then I wouldn’t be thinking quite as much, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think it would just disappear. Because the problem starts when I see it myself too. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it sort of locks in. Then even when I’m not actually with people, I’m replaying how I must have looked and imagining what everyone else noticed.\n\nSo if I were alone, maybe the panic about being watched would be lower in that moment. But the concern is still there because I’d still know it looks wrong to me, and I’d still be thinking about what people would see the next time I had to be around them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your face if you were certain that no one else noticed or cared about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, yes. Maybe it would take away part of it, the part where I’m imagining students or parents or staff staring at me instead of listening. If I could somehow know, like really know, that nobody noticed or cared, maybe I wouldn’t feel quite as exposed in front of people.\n\nBut the problem is that I notice it. That’s the part I can’t get around. I’m here because I keep seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it’s like I can’t unsee it. Even if someone told me, “No one else sees that,” I don’t know that I’d believe it fully, because to me it looks so obvious. It feels like it’s right there on the screen, and then my mind immediately goes to, “How could they not be looking at that?”\n\nSo… yes, I think I would still be concerned. Maybe I’d be less panicked about what other people think, but I’d still be stuck on how it looks to me. And then I’d probably still end up thinking about students, parents, and staff anyway, because if I can see it that clearly, it feels impossible that they wouldn’t be staring at it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the perceived flaw in your face isn't actually there, or that it's not as noticeable to others as you think it is?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know I’m supposed to say there’s a possibility, because logically I understand people might not be seeing it the way I am. I’m not completely unaware of that idea. But emotionally, when I’m looking at the lesson videos, it doesn’t feel questionable. It feels obvious.\n\nThat’s really why I’m here. I can’t stop seeing this one side of my face as crooked or swollen-looking, and once I notice it, it’s like everything else in the video disappears. I’m supposed to be looking at whether the lesson went well, or whether I explained something clearly, but instead I’m staring at my face and thinking, “That looks wrong. That’s what everyone must be seeing.”\n\nAnd then it turns into this whole loop where I imagine the students noticing it while I’m teaching, or parents noticing it if they watch something, or staff seeing it and focusing on that instead of anything I’m saying. So… could it be not as noticeable to others? Maybe. I can say maybe. But I don’t really feel convinced of that, because to me it looks so clear once I see it. That’s the problem — I can’t unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face is more about how you feel about your own appearance or about how others might evaluate you as a person because of it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being really honest, it starts with how I see myself. Like, I’m not first thinking, “They’ll think I’m a bad person,” exactly. It’s more that I see that one side of my face on the lesson videos and it looks crooked, or swollen, or just… wrong, and then I can’t move past it.\n\nBut then almost immediately it becomes about other people too. Because I’m a teacher, I’m in front of students all day, and there are parents and staff who might see me or see videos, and once I’ve noticed it, I feel like they must be noticing it too. I’ll think, “How could they be listening to what I’m saying if that’s what my face looks like?” It’s like I imagine them staring at that instead of focusing on the lesson.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s exactly that I think they’re judging my whole character because of it. It’s more like I feel exposed and embarrassed, like this thing on my face is so obvious that it takes over how people see me. Even if they’re not saying anything, I picture them noticing it, maybe being distracted by it, and then I can’t concentrate on anything else either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and re-record your lessons if you were the only person who would ever see them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably would, which is embarrassing to admit because if I’m the only one seeing it, then it should matter less, right? But I don’t think it would completely stop.\n\nWhen I’m recording lessons, before I post anything or even before something like a parent conference, I get stuck on how my face looks on the camera. I’ll adjust the angle over and over, like moving the laptop a little higher, then lower, then turning slightly to one side, trying to find the version where that side of my face doesn’t look so noticeable. And then I’ll replay little clips again and again, not really listening to what I’m teaching, but watching my face. Sometimes I’ll literally cover part of my face with my hand while I’m watching, just to see if it looks better or to compare what I think is wrong.\n\nSo if I were the only person who would ever see the video… I think I’d still check it, because I would still see it. Maybe I wouldn’t be as frantic about posting it, but I’d still feel this pull to fix it somehow or re-record it so it looks less awful to me. There have been times I’ve re-recorded the whole thing again and again because I can’t stand how it looks, and then I lose so much time that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to my next class. It becomes this whole thing where I tell myself, “Just one more take,” but it’s never just one more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you received reassurance from others that you look fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but I don’t think it would really fix it. Like, if someone said, “You look fine,” I might nod and try to believe them, and part of me would want that to be enough. But then the next time I see myself on a lesson video, I’m right back to staring at that side of my face and thinking it looks crooked or swollen-looking.\n\nIt’s not just that I need someone to tell me I’m okay. It’s that once I notice it, it feels so obvious to me that reassurance almost doesn’t land. I start thinking, “They’re just being kind,” or “Maybe they didn’t look closely,” or “They wouldn’t tell me if it really looked bad.” And then I imagine the students seeing it while I’m teaching, or parents or staff watching and focusing on that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo… I think reassurance might calm me down briefly, but it doesn’t stop me from seeing it. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t seem to get past that image of my face once it’s in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would still exist if you lived in complete isolation with no possibility of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I think… that’s hard to imagine, honestly. If I were completely isolated, with no students, no parents, no staff, nobody watching a lesson video or looking at me in a hallway, maybe part of the panic about other people noticing it would be less. I wouldn’t have that immediate thought of, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think the concern would disappear, because the first part of it is still me seeing it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and once I’ve seen it, it sort of becomes the only thing I can focus on. Even if no one else were around, I think I’d still look at my face and feel like something was wrong with it.\n\nThe social part makes it worse, definitely. Being a teacher makes it feel so exposed, like my face is right there in front of everyone and they must be noticing it. But even without that, I don’t think I’d suddenly feel normal about it. I think I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Being a teacher makes it feel so exposed, like my face is right there in front of everyone and they must be noticing it. But even without that, I don’t think I’d suddenly feel normal about it. I think I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your distress about your face is more related to the perceived flaw itself or the fear of what others might think of you because of it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with the flaw itself. Or what I think is the flaw. I’ll see my face on a lesson video and that one side just looks… crooked, or swollen-looking, and once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. It’s like everything else in the video disappears, even the actual teaching, and all I can see is that part of my face.

But then almost immediately it turns into the fear of other people seeing it too. Because I’m not just looking at myself in a mirror at home, I’m recording lessons, or I’m in front of students, or I’m dealing with parents and staff, and I start thinking, “They must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.” Like I’ll be explaining something and part of my mind is not even fully on the lesson because I’m imagining them noticing that side of my face.

So… I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. The distress is from me seeing it first, but it gets much worse because I feel exposed, like everyone else must see it the same way and be distracted by it or judging me for it.
