Because the problem starts when I see it myself too. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I see it, it sort of locks in. Then even when I’m not actually with people, I’m replaying how I must have looked and imagining what everyone else noticed.\n\nSo if I were alone, maybe the panic about being watched would be lower in that moment. But I don’t think it would completely stop.\n\nWhen I’m recording lessons, before I post anything or even before something like a parent conference, I get stuck on how my face looks on the camera. And then I’ll replay little clips again and again, not really listening to what I’m teaching, but watching my face. Sometimes I’ll literally cover part of my face with my hand while I’m watching, just to see if it looks better or to compare what I think is wrong.\n\nSo if I were the only person who would ever see the video… I think I’d still check it, because I would still see it. Maybe I wouldn’t be as frantic about posting it, but I’d still feel this pull to fix it somehow or re-record it so it looks less awful to me. There have been times I’ve re-recorded the whole thing again and again because I can’t stand how it looks, and then I lose so much time that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to my next class. It becomes this whole thing where I tell myself, “Just one more take,” but it’s never just one more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you received reassurance from others that you look fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute, but I don’t think it would really fix it. Like, if someone said, “You look fine,” I might nod and try to believe them, and part of me would want that to be enough. But then the next time I see myself on a lesson video, I’m right back to staring at that side of my face and thinking it looks crooked or swollen-looking.\n\nIt’s not just that I need someone to tell me I’m okay. It’s that once I notice it, it feels so obvious to me that reassurance almost doesn’t land. Because I’m not just looking at myself in a mirror at home, I’m recording lessons, or I’m in front of students, or I’m dealing with parents and staff, and I start thinking, “They must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.” Like I’ll be explaining something and part of my mind is not even fully on the lesson because I’m imagining them noticing that side of my face.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can separate them cleanly. But the main thing for me lately isn’t even a bathroom mirror so much as seeing myself on the lesson videos. That’s where it really hits me. I’ll watch it back and that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen-looking, and once I see it, it’s like it gets burned into my mind.\n\nSo if I somehow couldn’t see myself anywhere, including on videos, maybe the intensity would go down because I wouldn’t be confronted with it over and over. But I don’t know that the concern would just vanish, because once I’ve seen it, I carry it around. I’m teaching, or talking to students, or dealing with parents or staff, and I’m thinking, “They must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.”\n\nIt’s almost like the image follows me even when I’m not directly looking at myself. So removing mirrors might help a bit, but if I’m still being recorded or seen by other people, I think I’d still be preoccupied with it. The videos are the worst because they feel like proof, even if other people say it’s not noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would still cause problems in your daily life if you didn't care what others thought of your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would still cause problems, because at this point it’s not only about embarrassment or what people think. If I were completely isolated, with no students, no parents, no staff, nobody watching a lesson video or looking at me in a hallway, maybe part of the panic about other people noticing it would be less. I wouldn’t have that immediate thought of, “They’re staring at that side of my face instead of listening to me.”\n\nBut I don’t think the concern would disappear, because the first part of it is still me seeing it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and once I’ve seen it, it sort of becomes the only thing I can focus on. I’ll watch it back and that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen-looking, and once I see it, it’s like it gets burned into my mind.\n\nSo if I somehow couldn’t see myself anywhere, including on videos, maybe the intensity would go down because I wouldn’t be confronted with it over and over. I’m teaching, or talking to students, or dealing with parents or staff, and I’m thinking, “They must be looking at that side of my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.”\n\nIt’s almost like the image follows me even when I’m not directly looking at myself.
